# Athanasius of Alexandria, Third Oration against the Arians (Athanasius of Alexandria, Oratio III contra Arianos)

**Author**: Constantine of Preslav (translator)  
**Series**: PO Tome 58  
**Columns**: pp. 25–232  

[p. 25] {#po.58.p25}

### свѧтААгО А·ѳАНАсА НА АРИАны
Τοῦ αὐτοῦ κατὰ Ἀρειανῶν λόγος τρίτος
Of St. Athanasius,
*Арионєистовьнии. ꙗкоже мнѭ оуставивъшє си єдиіноѭ отъстѫпьници и прѣстѫпници быти отъ истины. тѧжѫтьсѧ въ сѧ въвлѣшти псаное,| єгда доидєть нечьстивыи въ глѫбинѫ ꙁълъ, неркадеть, ни обличаєми бо прѣстаѭть. ни недооумѣште оусрамѣѭтьсѧ. нъ ꙗкоже лицє блѫдница беꙁочьствоваша къ въсѣмъ въ нєчьстий. 1. Οἱ Ἀρειομανῖται, ὡς ἕοικε, κρίναντες ἅπαξ ἀποστάται γενέσθαι καὶ παρα βάται τῆς ἀληθείας, φιλονεικοῦσιν εἰς ἑαυτοὺς ἑλκῦσαι τὸ γεγραμμένον· Ὁταν ἔλθῃ ἀσεβὴς εἶς βάθος κακῶν, καταφρονεῖ· οὕτε γὰρ ἐλεγχόμενοι παύονται, οὕτε ἀποροῦντες ἐντρέπονται· ἀλλʼ ὡς πόρνης ὅψις, ἀπηναισχύνθησαν πρὸς πάντας ἐν ταῖς ἀσεβείαις.
Oration Three
нЕбѡІнъ имиже словєсы нєпьш̈тєвание имѣахѫ. єжє г҃ь съꙁьда мѧ. и тольма оуныи бывъ агтєлъ. и єжє прьвѣньць. и єжє вѣръна сѫшта сътворышоуꙋмѫ єго. правъ имѫштємъ раꙁоумъ. и оукаιꙁаѭштємъ благовѣриє єжє въ х҃а҃. нє вѣдѣ како пакы сами. ꙗкоⷤ сє ѻблиꙗни ѧдомъ змиинѡⷧ и не видѧште ихъже достоить видѣти. ни раꙁоумѣѭште же ихъже чьтѫть. ꙗкоⷤ иꙁъ глоуІбины нечьстивааго срьдьца своего отъригаѭІштє. начѧшѧ Καὶ γὰρ ὧν προεφασίζοντο ῥητῶν, τοῦ, Κύριος ἔκτισέ με, καὶ τοῦ, Κρείττων γενόμενος τῶν ἀγγέλων, καὶ τοῦ, Πρωτότοκος, καὶ τοῦ, Πιστὸν ὄντα τῷ ποιήσαντι αὐτὸν, ὀρθὴν ἐχόντων τὴν διάνοιαν, καὶ δεικνύντων τὴν εἶς Χριστὸν εὐσέβειαν οὖκ οἷδ᾽ ὅπως πάλιν αὐτοὶ ὡς περιχυθέντες τὸν τοῦ ὅφεως ἱ̓ὸν, καὶ μὴ βλέπο ντες ἃ δεῖ βλέπειν, μηδὲ νοοῦντες ἃ ἀναγινώσκουσιν, ὥσπερ ἐκ βάθους τῆς
1. 1. The Ario-maniacs, as | suppose, having once set on being the apostates and transgressors from the truth, strain to draw to themselves what is written, “When the impious comes into a depth of evils, he shows contempt” (Prov 18.3). For on being rebuked, they do not stop nor on being perplexed, do they become ashamed. But as having a “look of a prostitute”, they “act shamelessly” toward all in (their) impiety (Jer 3.3).
пакы тѧꙁати глаголємоє гоісподемъ. Аꙁъ въ отьци и отьць въ мнѣ · глаіголжштє, како можєть сь въ ономъ и онъ въ сємь въмѣштатисѧ. или како отънѫⷳ можєть отьць вѧштии сы, въ сынѣ мьншиі сѫшти въмѣштатисѧ. или коє чѫдно. аштє сынъ въ отци. а идєже и о насъ псано єⷭ҇ о нємь бо живємъ и движємьсѧ и єсмъ ἀσεβοῦς αὐτῶν καρδίας ἐρευγόμενοι, ἥρξαντο λοιπὸν καὶ διασύρειν τὸ ὑπὸ τοῦ Κυρίου λεγόμενον, Ἐγὼ ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, καὶ ὁ Πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοί· λέγοντες· Πῶς δύνατα οῦτος ἐν ἐκείνῳ, κὰκεῖνος ἐν τούτῳ χωρεῖν; Ἡ πῶς ὅλως δύναται ὁ Πατὴρ, μείζων ώ̀ν ἐν τῷ Υίῷ ἐλάττονι ὅντι χωρεῖν; Η τί θαομαστὸν, εὶ ὁ Υίὸς ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, ὅπουγε κα περὶ ἡμῶν γέγραπται· Ἐν αὐτῷ γὰρ ζῶμεν, καὶ κινούμεθα, καί ἐσμεν

дрыжать же се погод въ ꙁлооумѣнии своємъ ⁘ тѣло мьнѧштє сꙋште б҃а҃. и не раꙁоумѣваѭште, ни что єⷭ҇ истовъи отьц. и истовъи сынъ. ни что єсть свѣтъ нєвидимъ и присносѫшть. и сиꙗние его неівидимо. ни что єсть бєсплътьнъи съставъ. и наічрътаниє бєсплотно. и Обраꙁъ єго. аштє бо бишѧ вѣдѣли, не бышѧ г҃а҃ славы, тако съ смѣхомъ хоулили. ни бєсплътьнаѧ плътьскы раꙁоуімѣваѭштє. и глаголємаѧ добрѣ прѣтлъковаахоу. Πάσχουσι δὲ τοῦτο ἀκολούθως τῇ κακονοίᾳ ἑαυτῶν, σῶμα νομίζοντες εἶναι τὸν Θεὸν, καὶ μὴ νοοῦντες μήτε τί ἐστιν ἀληθινὸς Πατὴρ, καὶ ἀληθινὸς Y̓ἱὸς, μήτε τί ἐστι φῶς ἀόρατον, καὶ ἀῗδιον, καὶ ἀπαύγασμα αὐτοῦ ἀόρατον, μήτε τί ἐστιν ἀόρατος ὑπόστασις, καὶ χαρακτὴρ ἀσώματος, καὶ εἰκὼν ἀσώματος. Εἰ γὰρ ἐγίνωσκον, οὐκ ἃ́ν τὸν Κόριον τῆς δόξης μετὰ γέλωτος ἐδυσφήμουν, οὕτε τὰ ἀσώματα σωματικῶς ἐκλαμβάνοντες, τὰ καλῶς λεγόμενα παρεξηγοῦντο
довлѣашє оубо и тьчиѭ слышавъшємъ си глагоілѭшта г҃а вѣрѫ ꙗти. ꙁанєжє и простынѧ вѣра. оуньши єсть пытаньныѧ. єлмажє бо и сє каιꙁꙁиэти начаша. противѫ сво-є-рєси. ноужда єсты тѣхъ ꙁлооумѣниє Обличити. оумъ же истины оукаꙁати. ꙁа тврьдости бо дѣлма вѣрныих, Ηρκει μὲν οῦν καὶ μόνον ἀκούοντας ταῦτα, λέγοντος τοῦ Κυρίου, πιστεύειν ἐπεὶ καὶ ἡ τῆς ἁπλότητος πίστις βελτίων ἐστὶ τῆς ἐκ περιεργίας πιθανολογίας ἐπειδὴ δὲ καὶ τοῦτο βεβηλοῦν ἐπεχείρησαν πρὸς τὴν ἰδίαν αἵρεσιν, ἀναγκαῖον τὴν μὲν ἐκείνων κακόνοιαν διελέγξαι, τὴν δὲ τῆς ἀληθείας διάνοιαν δεῖξα ἕνεκά γε τῆς τῶν πιστῶν ἀσφαλείας.
нє ꙗкожє бо Они мнѧть. распрѧштєсѧ сѫть въ сєбѣ о глаголании, аꙁъ въ отьци и отыць въ мнѣ. ꙗкожє въ тъштихъ съсѫдѣ отꙑ самѣ сєбє

пакы жє ꙗкоже вꙑ свѧтый бываѧ б҃ъ оукрѣплѧєть ихъ такождє єсть и о сынѣ, самъ бо єсть отча сила и прѣмѫдрость. и бышьнаꙗ жє причастиємъ єго.| * О доусѣ свѧштаѭтсѧ. самъ жє сынъ. нє причѧстиємъ сынъ єсть. нъ пороⷩ отьчь свои єстъ. нѣістъ же пакы тако сынъ въ отци ꙗкоⷤ ѻ немь живеⷩ и движємсѧ и єсмъ. самъ бо ꙗкоⷤ иꙁъ источника оца еⷭ҇ животъ. О нємжє все оживлѧєтсѧ и състоитіѧ.! не живєт бо живо во животѣ. аште ли. то оуже нѣсть животъ, но самъ пачє животворить всє.| Οὐδ’ αὖ πάλιν, ὥσπερ ἐν τοῖς ἁγίοις γινόμενος ὁ Θεὸς, ἐνδυναμοῖ αὐτοὺς, οὕτως ἐστὶ καὶ ἐν τῷ Υἷῷ· αὐτὸς γάρ ἐστιν ἡ τοῦ Πατρὸς δύναμις καὶ σοφία· κα τὰ μὲν γενητὰ μετοχῆ τούτου ἐν Πνεύματι ἁγιάζεται· αὐτὸς δὲ ὁ Υἱὸς οὐ μετουσίς νἱός ἐστιν, ἀλλὰ γέννημα τοῦ Πατρὸς ἵδιόν ἐστιν. Οὐκ ἐ̔́στι δὲ πάλιν οὕτωςοδ̔ Οὐκ ἔστι δὲ πάλιν οὕτως ὁ Υἷὸς ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, ὡς τὸ, Ἐν αὐτῷ ζῶμεν, καὶ κινούμεθα, καί ἐσμεν· αὐτὸς γὰρ ὡς ἐκ πηγῆς τοῦ Πατρός ἐστιν ἡ ζωὴ, ἐν ᾗ τὰ πάντα ζωογονεῖταί τε καὶ σονέστηκεν οἰ γὰρ ἡ ζωὴ ἐν ζωῆ βῆ ἐπεὶ οὐκ ἂν εϊη ζωή· ἄλλ᾽αῦτὸς μᾶλλον ζωογονεῖ τὰ πάντα

Сє ꙋбо аштє би и простъ дѣтишть би съглаголалъ. Отьраⷧу би имѣлъ раштєниємь. нъ єлмаⷩ наричємъи прѣмѫдрьць, и всє творѧсѧ вѣды тъ̏ єсть написавъи. колика ꙁаꙁора достоинъ есть таковъи. какожє и тѫжда сєбє сѫшта апостола ꙗвлѧєть. вєличаѧсѧ иꙁобрѣтєнꙑ ими словесы прѣмѫдрости. и мнѧ тѣми въꙁмошти прѣльштати. нераꙁоумѣа самъ ихⷺже глаголєть, ни О чемь жє крѣпитсѧ. ꙗжє бо слоівєса сынъ своѧ. и оугодна єдиномѫ сынѫ. Слоівєси и прѣмѫдрости сѫштѫ. и ѡбраꙁѫ отчѧ єстьства иꙁдрєклъ єсть. та съ всѣх ꙁданииⷯ съносить, и обьшта сыновиⷧ и сиⷧⷩ҇ твориⷩ҇ ѧ./ * силѣ же глаголєть ѻтчи приимати силѫ ꙁаконоіпрѣстѫпьнъи. да въслѣдъ идєть своемоу неічьстиоу єже решти. ꙗко и сынъ о сынѣ въсынеінъ бысть. и слово словєси приꙗ власть "Ἀ γὰρ ὁ Yἱὸς ἵδια καὶ ἅρμόζοντα μόνῳ Υἱῷ, Λόγῳ καὶ Σοφίᾳ ὅντι, καὶ εἰκόν τῆς τοῦ Πατρὸς οὐσίας εἴρηκε, ταῦτα οὗτος εἰς πάντα τὰ κτίσματα καταφέρει καὶ κοινὰ τῷ τε Yἱῷ καὶ τούτοις ταῦτα ποιεῖ· τὴν δὲ δύναμιν τοῦ Πατρὸς λέγε λαμβάνειν δύναμιν ὁ παράνομος, ἵνα ἀκολουθήση τῇ δυσσεβείᾳ αὐτοῦ εἰπεῖν ὅτ καὶ ὁ Yἱὸς ἐν Yἵῷ υἱοποιήθη, καὶ ὁ Λόγος ἐ̔́λαβε Λόγου ἐξουσίαν
и к тоlмоу оуже не хоштеть ꙗкоⷤ сынѫ си иꙁдрєкшоу.| нъ ꙗкоже и томѫ оувѣдѣвъшѫ такожде приічитаєть съ всѣми тварьми · Да аштє не бѣішѧ словєса сыновьнѧ ꙗжє иꙁъглагола, нъ ꙗко отчѧ бѣшѧ и дѣла. глаголалъ ли би аꙁъ въ ѻт҇ци, и ѻтьць въ мнѣ. глаголєть же и д҃ад · Да оуіслышѫ что глаголєть въ мнѣ г҃ь б҃ь. Салѡмонъ же, моѧ словєса рєчєна сѫть отъ бога и мѡѵ̈іси жє словєса ꙗжє отъ бога ꙁприношаше,, къжьдо жє отъ проірокъ, не своѧ нъ ꙗжє отъ б҃а глаголаашє· сє καὶ οὐκ ἔτι μὲν αὐτὸν, ὠ̔ς Yἱὸν, θέλει εἰρηκέναι ταῦτα, ὡς δὲ μαθόντα κο αὐτὸν ὁμοίως συντάσσει πᾶσι τοῖς ποιήμασιν. Εἶ γὰρ διὰ τὸ μὴ εἶναι τὰ ῥήματε τοῦ Yίοῦ, ἃ διεξήρχετο, ἀλλʼ ὅτι τοῦ Πατρὸς ῆν, καὶ τὰ ἔργα, ἕλεγεν· Ἔγὼ ἐν τα Πατρὶ, καὶ ὁ Πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοί καὶ ὁ μὲν Δαβὶδ λέγει Ἀκούσομαι τί λαλήσει ἐν ἐμο Κύριος ὁ Θεός· ὁ δὲ Σολομών· Οἵ ἐμοὶ λόγοι είρηνται ὑπὸ Θεοῦ· καὶ ὁ μὲν Μωσι τοὺς παρὰ τοῦ Θεοῦ διηκόνει λόγους, ἕκαστος δὲ τῶν προφητῶν οὐ τὰ ἵδια

Ἀλλ’ εΐπερ ἧν οὕτως ὁ Κύριος εἰρηκὼς, ἕδει μὴ εἰπεῖν αὐτόν Ἐγὼ ἐν τᾷ Πατρὶ, καὶ ὁ Πατὴρ ἐν ἐμοί· ἀλλὰ μᾶλλον· Κάγὼ ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, καὶ ὁ Πατὴρ δὲ καὶ ἐν ἐμοί ἐστιν, ἵνα μηδὲ ἴδιον καὶ ἐξαίρετον ἐ̔́χῃ πρὸς τὸν Πατέρα ὡς Yἱὸς, κοινὴν δὲ μετὰ πάντων ἔχη τὴν αὐτὴν χάριν. 3. And this they hold consistent with their evil-mindedness, thinking that God is a (material) body and not understanding what is “true Father” and “true Son”, nor what is “light invisible” and “eternal” and its “radiance invisible”, nor what is “immaterial subsistence” and “immaterial expression” and his “image” (Heb 1.3; Col 1.15). For if they knew, they would not blaspheme (and) ridicule the Lord of glory, nor understanding immaterial in the material sense, would they pervert what is spoken well.

2. [+ The Son is in the Father gr.], inasmuch as we can know, because everything that belongs to the Son is proper to the Father's essence, as radiance from light, and river from spring, so that whoever sees the Son, sees his own Father and understands that the Son’s being, in virtue of (coming) from the Father, is therefore in the Father.
And the Father is in the Son because that which is properly from the Father is the Son, as in the radiance the sun, and in the word the thought, and in the river the spring. Thus, whoever sees the Son, sees what is proper to the Father's essence and understands that the Father is in the Son.
3. For since the form [+ and gr.] Godhead of the Father is the being of the Son, it is necessary that the Son be in the Father and the Father in the Son. For on this account and with the reason, having first said, “| and the Father are one” (Jn 10.30), he added the phrase “| am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10), in order to show the identity of Godhead and the unity of essence

4. 1. For they are one not as one thing divided afterward into two parts, and these nothing but one, nor as one thing twice named, so that the same becomes at one time Father, at another his own Son, for this (is what) Sabellius thought (and) was judged a heretic. But they are two (in number) because the Father is Father and is not also Son, and the Son is Son and not also Father. But the nature is one because the offspring is not unlike its parent, for it is his image, and all that is the Father's is the Son’s.
2. Therefore, neither is the Son another God, for he was not procured from without, or else there would be many (gods), if a godhead is to be procured foreign to the Father's. For even though the Son is other as offspring, he is still the same as God. And he and the Father are one in propriety and peculiarity of nature, and in the identity of the one Godhead, as has been said.

вьсь свои ємоу породъ. таковъи жє породъ, свѣтъ єсть. и никтоⷤ нє рєчеⷮ҇ дъва свѣта ѧ сѫшта, нъ дваⷤ, слънцє и сиꙗниє. Единъ же свѣтъ иже отъ слъньца. въ сиꙗнїи просвѣштаѧ вьсе. Καὶ γὰρ καὶ τὸ ἀπαύγασμα φῶς ἐστιν, οὐ δεύτερον τοῦ ἡλίου, οὐδὲ ἕτερον φῶς, οὐδὲ κατὰ μετουσίαν αὐτοῦ, ἀλλ᾽ ὅλον ΐδιον αὐτοῦ γέννημα. Τὸ δὲ τοιοῦτον γέννημα ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἕν ἐστι φῶς· καὶ οὐκ ἄν τις εἵποι δύο φῶτα εῖναι ταῦτα, ἀλλὰ δύο μὲν ἥλιον καὶ ἀπαύγασμα, ἑν δὲ τὸ ἐξ ἡλίου φῶς ἐν τῷ ἀπαυγάσματι φωτίζον τὰ πανταχοῖ
3. For the radiance too is light, not second to the sun, nor different light, nor by participation of it, but a whole (and) proper offspring of it. Such an offspring is [+ necessarily one gr.] light, and no one would say that they are two lights, but while sun and radiance are two, yet there is one light from the sun enlightening in its radiance everything.
4. In the same way, the Son’s Godhead is the Father's, and, therefore, it is also indivisible, and thus, there is one God and there is none other but he. And so, (since) they are one and the Godhead is one, the same thing that is said of the Son is also said of the Father, except when he is said to be “Father”. For example, he is God: “And the Word was God” (Jn 1.1); Almighty: “Thus says he who was and is and is to come, the Almighty” (Rev 1.8); he is Lord: “One Lord Jesus Christ” (1 Cor 8.6); he is light: “I am the light” (Jn 8.12); he wipes out sins: “So that we may know”, he says, “that the Son of man has authority on earth to forgive sins” (Lk 5.24). And you will find this with other (attributes). For “everything”, says the Son himself, “that the Father has is mine” (Jn 16.15), and again, “mine is yours” (Jn 17.10).

2. [+ And being the proper offspring of the Father's essence, the Son gr] in truth speaks of the Father's attributes as his own. Therefore, it is fitting and consistent with
saying, “| and the Father are one” (Jn 10.30), when he added “that you may know that | am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 10.38), and before this, he also said, “He who has seen me, has seen the Father” (Jn 14.9). And there is one and the same
sense in these three statements. For anyone who understands this way that the Son and the Father are one, knows that he is in the Father and the Father in the Son, for the Godhead of the Son is the Father's, and it is in the Son. And whoever grasped this, believes that “he who has seen the Son, has seen the Father” (Jn 14.9), because the Godhead of the Father is seen in the Son.

Διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ἵσα Θεῷ ὢν, οὐλ ἁρπαγμὸν ἡγήσατο τὸ εἶναι ἵσα Θεῷ· καὶ πάλιν ἐπειδὴ τοῦ Yῖοῦ ἡ θεότης καὶ τὸ εἶδος οὐδενὸς ἅλλου, ἣ τοῦ Πατρός ἐστι, τοῦτό ἐστιν, ὅπερ εῖπε τὸ, Ἐγὼ ἐν τῷ Πατρι
6. Nor again, as being in the saints (when) God strengthens them, thus is he also in the Son. For he is himself the Father's power and Wisdom, and by partaking of him the originate things are sanctified in the Spirit. But the Son himself is not son by participation, but is the Father’s own offspring. 7. Nor again is the Son in the Father in the same sense as we are (when it is said), “In him we live and move and have our being” (Acts 17.28). For he, as being from the fount of the Father is the “life” (Jn 14.6), in which everything is quickened and consists (Col 1.17). For the life does not live in life, or else it would no longer be life, but rather, he himself gives life to everything (1 Tim 6.13).
5. And he who worships the image, in it worships the king, for the image is his form and appearance. Now, since the Son is the image of the Father, is it not easy to understand that the Godhead and propriety of the Father is the being of the Son? And that is (the meaning of what is said): “He who being in the form of God” (Phil 2.6), and “the Father in me” (Jn 10.38).
6. 1. Nor is the form of the Godhead partial, but the fullness of the Father's Godhead (Col 2.9) is the being of the Son, and the Son is wholly God. That is why, being equal to God, he “did not launch [did not consider it gr.] a robbery to be equal to God” (Phil 2.6). And again, since the Godhead and form of the Son is none other's but the Father's, this is what he said, “I am in the Father” (Jn 14.10).

2. Thus, “God was in Christ reconciling the world to himself’ (2 Cor 5.19). For the Son is proper to the Father's essence and in him work was reconciled with God. Thus, the things that are done by the Son are the Father's deeds (Jn 10.37), for the Son is the form of the Father's Godhead, which does the deeds. So, he who sees the Son sees the Father, for in the Father's Godhead is and is seen the Son, and the Father's form that is in him shows in him the Father, and thus the Father is in the Son. And that propriety and Godhead that is from the Father [+ in gr.] the Son, shows the Son in the Father and always inseparable from him.
3. And he who hears and sees that what is said of the Father is also said of the Son — not as accruing to his essence by grace nor by participation, but because the very being of the Son is the proper offspring of the Father's essence, — will understand the saying well, as | said, “| am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10), and “I and the Father are One” (Jn 10.30). For the Son is such as the Father because he has everything that is the Father's.

5. And on cleansing him from leprosy (4 Kings 5.8-15), Elisha says to Naaman, “that you may know that there is a God in Israel” (1 Kings 17.46); and Samuel too in the days of the harvest prayed to God to grant rain (1 Kings 12.18); and the apostles were saying that they did miracles not in their own power but in the Lord’s grace (e.g. Acts 3.12). It is clear that this statement is common to them (all), so that each of them would be able to say, “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10). And, therefore, he is not one Son of God and Word and Wisdom, but one out of many.

But if the Lord did say (that) in this sense, it would fit him to say not “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10), but rather “both | am in the Father and the Father in me’, so that he may have nothing of his own, nor any exceptional relation toward the Father, as a Son, but the same grace common with all. But it is not said in the sense they think (it is). For not holding that he is the genuine Son from the Father, they slander the genuine one, whom alone befits to say, “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10).

Ἐπειδὴ δὲ οἱ ἀσεβεῖς καὶ ταῦτα φέροντες δυσφημοῦσι μὲν τὸν Κύριον ὀνειδίζουσι δὲ ἡμῖν λέγοντες· Ἰδοὺ εῖς, καὶ μόνος, καὶ πρῶτος λέγεται ὁ Θεὸς πῶς ὑμεῖς λέγετε τὸν Yίὸν εἶναι Θεόν; Εὶ γὰρ ἧν Θεὸς, οὐκ ὰ́ν ἕλεγεν, Εγὼ μόνος
7. And he too is the first (as) the fullness of the Godhead of the first and only, being wholly and fully God. Therefore, it is not said on his account, but to reject (the fact that) there is another like the Father and his Word. That this is the sense of the prophet is clear and apparent to all.
6. And because of that, there is one honor and one worship, (rendered) to the Father in and through the Son. And he who worships this way, worships one God, for God is one and none other but he. And when the Father is spoken as the only God, and (when it is said that) there is one God, and “I am” (Ex 3.14), and “beside me there is no God” (Deut 32.39), and “I am the first and | am the last” (Isa 44.6), this is stated well, for God is one and only [+ and gr|] first. But this is not said for the rejection of the Son. No way! For he is in that one and first and only, as being of that one and only and first the only Word and Wisdom and radiance.

But if he is the Father's Son, who would be so insane, except for the Christ-contenders, so as to think that God spoke such words for disgracing and denying his own Word?
This is not a Christian meaning (of those words). No way! These things are written not with reference to the Son, but for the rejection of those falsely-called gods, made by men. And the meaning of such statements is (made clear) by a reasonable response.

3. Recognizing this, John has indicated (it) by writing in the Epistle, “And we are in (him who is) true, in his Son Jesus Christ. He is the true God and eternal life” (1 Jn 5.20). And when the prophet says about the creation that stretches forth the heavens alone (Job 9.8), and when God (says), “I alone stretched out the heavens” (Isa 44.24), it is clear to all that in the only one is also implied the Word of the only, in whom “everything came to be”, and “without him nothing was made” (Jn 1.3). Therefore, if (they) were made through the Word, and yet he says, “I alone”, and together with this “alone one” is understood the Son, through whom the heavens were made, so also then if it is to be said, “one God”, and “I alone”, and “I am the first”, in that one and alone and first is understood the coexisting Word, as in the light the radiance.

If then it is on this account that the Son and the Father are one, and if it is in this way that the Word is allowed to be like the Father, then it follows that the angels too, and the other beings that are above us — that is, first principles, and powers, and authorities, and thrones, and dominions (Col 1.16), — and things visible — that is, sun, and moon, and the stars, — should be sons also, as the Son; and of them too it should be said that they and the Father are one, and that each is the Father’s image and Word. For what the Son wills, that same thing they will also, so that they defer neither in judging nor in thinking, but in everything are obedient to the one who made them. For they would not have remained in their glory, unless what the Father willed, that same thing they had willed also. The one who did not remain but became insane, heard (the words), “How have you fallen from heaven, oh day star of the rising morning”? (Isa 14.12).

2. [+ And gr_] in what way would he be different from [+ the Father gr.], given that the Father is Father, but the regulations and the teaching is the Father's? Now, if the Son is like the Father in respect to the regulations and the teaching, then the Father, according to them, will be Father in name only. And the Son will not be an unchanging image but rather will appear to have neither propriety, nor any likeness of the Father at all. For what likeness and propriety is there, given the fact that the difference is with no other but the Father, who (always) exists?

3. And he who beholds the angelic vision, knows that he has seen the angel, and not God (Lk 24.23). For Zechariah saw an angel (Zech 1.9), and Isaiah saw the Lord (Isa 6.1). An angel appeared to Manoah, the father of Samson (Judg 13.21), and God appeared to Abraham (Gen 26.24; Acts 7.2). And neither he who saw God, saw an angel, nor he who saw an angel, though that he saw God, because things originate by nature are greatly distanced from God who made them.

4. But if at any time, when the angel was seen, he who saw it, heard God's voice, as it happened at the bush. For the angels of the Lord appeared in a flame of fire out of the bush, and the Lord called Moses out of the bush, saying, “| am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob” (Ex 3.2, 4, 6), yet it was not the angel the God of Abraham, but in the angels there was God.
6. And what the Son gives, that is the giving of the Father. And he who saw the Son, knows that in seeing him, he saw not angel, nor anyone greater than angels, nor merely a creature, but saw the Father himself (Jn 14.9). And he who hears the Word, knows that he hears the Father, as he who is irradiated by the radiance, knows that he is enlightened by the sun.

6. (They are) pitiful men, whose trouble is even greater from thinking against Christ, for they fell from the truth and surpassed the Jews in (their) treachery by rejecting Christ. And they wallow with the Greeks, God-haters as they are, serving the creature and different gods.
8. And having seen this too, the patriarch Jacob received a blessing and was named by him Israel instead of Jacob, as divine Scriptures testify, saying, “The sun rose upon him when he passed by the form of God” (Gen 32.31). And it was that (very form) that
said, “He who has seen me has seen the Father” (Jn 14.9), and “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10), and “I and the Father are one” (Jn 10.30). For thus God is one, and there is one faith in the Father and in the Son. For while the Word is God, again, “the Lord our God is one Lord” (Deut 6.4). For the Son is proper to, and inseparable from, that one according to the propriety and peculiarity of his essence.

7. For there is one God, and not many, [+ and one is his Word, and not many gr]. And the Word is God (Jn 1.1), (and) he alone has the form of the Framer. Being then such, the Savior himself restrained the Jews, saying, “The Father himself who sent me, has himself testified about me. You neither heard his voice at any time nor seen his form; and you do not have his Word abiding in you, for whom he has sent, him you did not believe” (Jn 5.37-38). He has joined the “Word” to the “form” in a good way, so that he may show that the Word of God is his image and expression and form of his Father; and that (since) the Jews did not receive him who spoke to them, they did not receive the Word, which is the form of God.

4. It is a custom with divine Scriptures to take the things of nature as images and illustrations for men. And this they do, so that from these natural phenomena the moral dynamic of man may be explained, and thus their manner (of conduct) shown to be either deceptive or righteous. In the case of the deceptive, as when it puts a prohibition, “Do not be like the horse or the mule which have no understanding” (Ps 31.9). Or when it says, rebuking those who have become such, “Man, being in honor, has no understanding; he is compared to the senseless cattle, and is like to them” (Ps 48.13).

And he says this too, again, not that we might become [+ such as the Father gr.], for to become as the Father is impossible due to us being creatures and being brought into being out of nothing, but (it is rather) as he commanded, “Do not be like the horse” (Ps 31.9), not that we should become like cattle, but that we should not imitate their cattle-like senselessness, 2. so, not that we might become as God, did he say, “Be merciful as your Father” (Lk 6.36), but that seeing his kind acts, whatever good we do, we might do not for men’s sake, but for his sake, so that not from him and not from men we may have the reward.

not by being made equal to God, nor becoming in nature and truth merciful — for it is not our thing to benefit, but God’s, (and) in us this happens by grace — but in order that what has come to us from God himself by grace, these things we too may offer to others, without being arrogant, but simply toward all extending (our) mercy. For only in this way can we somehow become his imitators, and in no other way, but when we give to others what comes from him.

(And) how could this be when he is God’s Word and Wisdom, while we were formed out of the earth? And he is by nature and essence Word and true God, for that is what John said, “We know that the Son of God has come and has given us an understanding, so that we may know the one who is true; and we are in him who is true, in his Son Jesus Christ” (1 Jn 5.20). 2. For he immediately added, saying, “I am in them and you in me, so that they may be made perfect in one” (Jn 17.22-23). Here the Lord is now asking something greater and more perfect for us, for it is clear that the Word has come to be in us because he clothed himself in our body: “‘And you Father in me’ (Jn 17.21), for | am your Word, and since you are in me, because | am your Word, and | in them because of the body, salvation of men is perfected in me through you, (and) | ask that they too may become one, according to the body that is on me and according to its perfection; that they too may become perfect, having oneness with it, and having become one in it; that, as if all being clothed in me, all may be one body and one spirit, and may reach ‘a perfect man” (Eph 4.4, 13). For we all, partaking of the same, become one body, having the one Lord in ourselves.

Οὐκοῦν ὁ Σωτὴρ λέγων περὶ ἡμῶν τὸ, Καθὼς σὺ, Πάτερ, ἐν ἐμοὶ, κά̓γὼ ἐν σοὶ, ἵνα καὶ αὐτοὶ ἐν ἡμῖν ἑ͂ν ὦσιν, οὐ τὴν ταυτότητα ἡμᾶς αὐτῷ μέλλοντας ἔχειν σημαίνει· ἐδείχθη γὰρ ἐκ τοῦ κατὰ τὸν Ἰωνᾶν παραδείγματος καὶ τοῦτο· ἀλλὸ ἀξίωσίς ἐστι πρὸς τὸν Πατέρα, ὡς ὁ Ἰωάννης ἔγραψεν, ἵνα τὸ Πνεῦμα χαρίσητα δι’ αὐτοῦ τοῖς πιστεύουσι, δι’ οὗ καὶ δοκοῦμεν ἐν τῷ Θεῷ γίνεσθαι, καὶ κατὸ τοῦτο συνάπτεσθαι ἐν αὐτο͂
25. 1. Therefore, the Savior speaking of us, “As you, Father, are in me, and | in you, that they too may be one in us” (Jn 17.21), signifies not that we were to have identity with him — for this was indicated from the example concerning Jonah, — but a prayer to the Father, as John wrote, that the Spirit would be offered through him to those who believe, through whom we also consider to be in God, and in that way be united with him.

Ельмаⷩ бо слово єсть въ отьци. а доухъ Єльмаⷩ бо слово єсть въ отьци. а доухъ доухъ. да єгда словєсємъ даєтъсѧ. хоштеⷩⷮ приꙗти намъ словєсємъ даєтъсѧ. хоштеⷮ приꙗти намъ доухъ. да єгда и приимемъ. тъгда иімѫште доухъ словєси сѫштоу въ отци. и мы ꙁа доуха дѣлъма бѫдємъ въ словєси. и тѣмъ отцєви. 5. Upon this again, these pitiful men say, “If the Son were, according to your understanding, eternally existent with God, he had not been ignorant of the day, but had known as Word; nor had been forsaken as being coexistent; nor had asked to receive glory, as having it in the Father; nor prayed at all, for, being the Word, he had required nothing, but since he is a creature and one of things originate, for that reason, he spoke in such a way and needed what he did not have. For it is proper to creatures to require and ask what they do not have”.

6. For being the Son, he is inseparable from the Father, and there was no time when he was not, but he was always (there), and being the Father's image and radiance, he has the Father's eternity. Now, (the extent) to which they have been rebuked for misunderstanding the passages, which they have alleged, is possible to see from what has been briefly said before. And that things to which they refer again from the Gospels, they appear to understand as having a rotten sense, we may easily see, if we now again take (into consideration) the sense of our Christian faith, and holding it as a rule, apply ourselves, as the apostle said, to the honorable [to the reading of gr.] inspired Scripture (1 Tim 4.13; 2 Tim 3.16). For Christ-contenders, not understanding this, have gone astray from the path of truth, and “have stumbled on the stumbling rock” (Rom 9.33), (pretending to) have more wisdom than the (actual) wisdom they have (Rom 12.3).

31. 1. Of old times then he used to come to each of the saints and always sanctify those who receive him. But neither when they were born was it said that he had been born man [he had become man gr.], nor when they suffered, was it said that he himself suffered. But when he came (as) man from Mary “once at the end of the ages for the abolition of sin” (Heb 9.26) — for thus pleased the Father “to send his own Son born of a woman, made under the law” (Gal 4.4), — then it is said that he, taking flesh, became man and in that (flesh) suffered for us — as Peter says, “Christ then suffered in the flesh” (1 Pet 4.1), — so that it may be shown and that all might believe that while he was always God, and sanctified those to whom he came, and embellished everything according to the Father's will, afterward for our sake he also became man, and “bodily” (Col 2.9), as the apostle says, the Godhead dwelt in the flesh, (which) equals to saying, “Being God, he had his own body, and having it as an instrument, he became man’.

And the prophet said it well, “bore” (Isa 53.4, 11; Mt 8.17), and did not say “He has healed our infirmities”, lest, as being outside of the body, and only healing (it), as he has always done, he should leave men still subject to death. But he bores our infirmities, and he himself bears our sins, that it might be shown that he has become man for our sake, and that the body which in him bore them, was his own body.

3. If then the body had been someone else’s, to him too had been the affections ascribed, but if the flesh is the Word's — for “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14), — of necessity (then) the affections of the flesh are also attributed to him, whose flesh itis.
4. And to whom the affections are attributed — such as to be condemned, to be beaten, to thirst; and the crucifixion, and death, and other bodily infirmities — of him too is the improvement and grace. On account of this then, it is fitting and suitable to attribute such affections to no one else but the Lord, so that the grace also may be from him, and that we may become not ministers of any other but truly be devout toward God, because we call (for) no originate thing, no simple man, but the natural and true Son from God, who also became man, yet is no less than Lord and God and Savior.

34. 1. But that everyone may begin to understand the truth and the impassible nature of the Word and the infirmities attributed to him in view of the flesh, it is well to listen to the blessed Peter, for he will be a worthy witness about the Savior. He writes then in the Epistle, saying, “Christ then having suffered for our sake in the flesh” (1 Pet 4.1).

3. For in view of this, the apostle himself did not say, “Christ then having suffered” in his Godhead, but “for us in the flesh” (1 Pet 4.1), in order to show that all these things are proper not to the very Word by nature, but (that) affections are proper by nature to the very flesh. Let no one then stumble at things that pertain to man, but rather let him know that in nature the Word himself is impassible, yet because of the flesh, in which he clothed himself, these things are said of him, since it all is proper to the flesh, and the body is proper to the Savior.

4. But he himself continues to be impassible in nature, remaining as he is (and) having received no harm from these (affections), but rather eliminating them and destroying them. Men, on the other hand, as if having their affections transferred to the impassible and abolished, themselves now become impassible and free from them for the ever, as John taught, saying, “And you know that he was manifested in order to take away our sins, and there is no sin on him” (1 Jn 3.5).

35. 1. These (things) then we (considered) necessary to examine first, so that when we see him doing or saying (anything) divinely through his own body, as an instrument, we may know that he does such things being God, and again, if we see him speaking or suffering humanly, we may not be ignorant that he carried flesh (and) became man, and hence he does and speaks that way. For when we acknowledge what is proper to each, and see and understand that both (these things and those other) are done by One, we believe rightly and will never go astray. For “the Father loves the Son and gave him everything into his hand” (Jn 3.35); and “everything was given to me from my Father” (Mt 11.27); and “I can do nothing of myself, but as | hear, | judge” (Jn 5.30); and all other such statements show not that the Son had never had all these things — for how what the Father has, the only Word and Wisdom of the Father in essence did not have eternally, (the one) who said, “All that the Father has is mine” and mine is the Father's? (Jn 16.15; 17.10). For if the things of the Father are the Son's, and the Father has them always, it is clear that what the Son has, being the Father's, was always in him (i.e. the Son) — 5. not then because he had never had them, did he say this, but because, while the Son has eternally what he has, yet he has them from the Father.

36. 1. But let no one on seeing that the Son has all that the Father has, from the unvarying likeness and identity of that which he has, go astray as the impious Sabellius or consider him to be the Father, taking into account (such) statements (as), “was given to me” (Mt 28.18), and “I received” (Jn 10.18), and “was delivered to me” (Mt 11.27). Let these (statements) alone show him that he is not the Father, but the Father's Word, and the proper [eternal gr.] Son, who distinctly [eternally gr.] has what he has from him (i.e. Father) on account of his likeness to the Father, and because he is the Son, he has from the Father what he has distinctly [eternally gr.].

2. And that the phrases “was given” and “was delivered” and statements similar to these do not impair the Godhead of the Son, but rather show him to be truly Son can be learned from the passages themselves. For if “everything is delivered to him” (Mt 11.27), first, he is different from all that he received; then, being heir of everything, he alone is the Son and proper by nature to the Father. For if he were one of all, then he would not be “heir of all” (Heb 1.2), but everyone had received as the Father willed and gave. But now as himself receiving everything, he is other than them all, and alone [+ proper gr.] to the Father.

3. But the fact that the phrases “was given’, [+ and “was delivered” gr.] do not show that once he did not have them, can be seen from a similar passage and in like manner concerning them all. Now, from what he said “has given”, he shows that he is not the Father, but in saying “so”, he shows the natural property of the Son toward the Father and the likeness.

3. Thus, John, when asking the Lord, “How many (loaves of) bread do you have"? (Mk 6.38), was not ignorant, but knew, for he says, “And this he said to test Philip, for he himself knew what he would want to do” (Jn 6.6). But if he knew what he was doing, [+ then not in ignorance, but with knowledge did he ask gr.]. From this we may understand similar (instances), that when the Lord asks, he does not ask in ignorance where Lazarus lies, nor (is this the case) when he asks who people say he is, but (he acts) knowing what he was asking, being aware of what he himself wanted to do. And soon, thus, the cleverness of their point will be destroyed.

4. But if they still argue on account of his asking, let them hear that in the Godhead there is no ignorance, but to the flesh it is proper not to know, as has been said. And that this is truth, notice how the Lord, when asking where Lazarus lay, himself said, while being not there but far away, “Lazarus is dead” (Jn 11.14), and (told) where he died. And he who is thought to be ignorant, according to them, is he himself who foreknew the thoughts of the disciples (Mt 16.8), and knew what was in the heart of each and “what is in man” (Jn 2.25), and what is greater. He alone knows the Father (Jn 10.15) and says, “l am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10).

38. 1. Therefore, it is clear to all that it is proper to the flesh not to know, whereas the Word himself, or [whereas the Word himself considered as gr.] the Word, knows everything even before it comes to be. For when he became man, he did not cease to be God, nor since he is God, does he avoid what is human. No way! But rather, being God, he took flesh and, being in the flesh, he deified flesh.

3. And when then the Savior uses the words which they allege as a pretext for their point, “power is given to me” (Mt 28.18), and “glorify your Son” (Jn 17.1), and Peter saying, “power is given to him” (Rev 13.7), we understand all these statements in the same sense, that humanly, because of the body, he says all this. For though he had no need, yet he is said to have received what he received humanly, so that again, insofar as the Lord has received, and insofar as the gift rests in him, the grace will remain firm.

grace may not be lost and may be firmly secured by men, for that reason he himself makes the gift his own. And he says that he received power as man, which he always had as God. And he says, “Glorify me”, who glorifies others, to show that he has a flesh that requires such things. Therefore, when the flesh receives, since that which receives is on him, and by taking it, he has become man, he is thus said himself to have received.

[+ For gr.] after saying, “Nor the Son” (Mk 13.32), he describes to the disciples what precedes the day, saying, “This and that will be, and then the end” (Mt 24.14). But he who speaks of what precedes the day, surely knows the day also, which will come according to what was foretold. But if he had not known the hour, he had not spoken of things that would come after him, without knowing when that hour will be.

5. (This can be compared to) anyone, who wants to point to a house or city (in helping) those who do not know (where) it is: he discloses what comes before the house or city, and having indicated everything, he says, “Right after that (place) there will be the city or the house”. The one who gives such indications certainly knows where the house or the city is, for had he not known, he had not given indications regarding the things that were said, lest somehow from ignorance he should either throw his hearers far out of the way, or in speaking he should become forgetful and go beyond what is indicated. In the same way, the Lord, saying what precedes the day and hour, knows with precision, nor is ignorant of when (that) hour and day will come.

2. And this, again, everyone will see well if he examines with good mind the occasion, when and to whom the Savior spoke these things. Not then when the heaven was made by him, nor when the Word was with the Father himself, pleasing (him) in everything, nor before he became man did he say that, but when “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14). For that reason, it is correct to attribute to his manhood everything which, after he became man, he speaks humanly.

3. And everyone except the Arians would express the same thing, that he who knows the Father, much more knows the whole of the creation, and in that whole its end also. And if already the day and the hour be determined by the Father, it is clear that this is determined through the Son, and he knows himself what has been determined through him, for there is nothing that has come to be and has been determined except through the Son. Therefore, he, being the Framer of everything, knows what and how much and to what extent the Father commanded it to be made in sucha way; and how much and to what extent it will have its diversity.

4. And again, the example from the time of Noah rebukes the shamelessness of Christ-contenders, for there too he did not say, “I did not understand”, but “they did not understand until the flood came” (Mt 24.39). For men did not know, but he who brought the flood — and it was the Savior himself — knew the day and the hour in which he opened the heavenly streams and disclosed the abyss, and said to Noah, “Go into the ark, you and your sons” (Gen 7.1).

5. For if he had been ignorant, he had not foretold to Noah, “For in seven more days | will bring rain on the earth” (Gen 7.4). But if with the help of the parallel of Noah's time he signifies the day, and he did know the day of the flood, then he knows also the day of his own coming.

46. 1. And after telling the parable of the virgins, again he shows more clearly who they are who does not know the day and the hour, when he says, “But be alert because you know neither the day nor the hour” (Mt 25.13). He who said shortly before, “No one knows, nor the Son” (Mk 13.32), now says not “I do not know’, but “you do not know”.

Καὶ τὴν κατὰ τὰς παρθένους δὲ ὁμοίωσιν εἰρηκὼς, φανερώτερον πάλιν ἕδειξε, τίνες εἰσὶν οἱ ἀγνοοῦντες τὴν ἡμέραν καὶ τὴν ὥραν, λέγων, Γρηγορεῖτε οὖν, ὅτι οὐκ οἵδατε τὴν ἡμέραν ἢ τὴν ὥραν, ὁ πρὸ ὀλίγου λέγων, Οὐδεὶς οἶδεν, οὐδὲ ὁ Yἷὸς, νῦν οὐκ εἶπεν, ὅτι Ἐγὼ οὐκ οἶδα, ἀλλ᾽ ὅτι Ὑμεῖς οὐκ οἴδατε.
2. In this way then, when his disciples asked about the end, he said it well then, “Nor the Son” (Mk 13.32), according to the flesh, because of the body, so that he might show that he does not know as man, for it belongs to men to not know. If then he is the Word, and he is the one who is to come (Heb 10.37), and he is the judge, and he is the bridegroom (Mt 25.1), he knows when and in what hour he comes, and he is to say, “Wake up, sleeper, and rise from the dead” (Eph 5.14). For as, on becoming man, he hungers and thirsts and suffers with man, so also with men as man he does not know, whereas divinely, being in the Father (as) Word, he knows, and there is nothing that he does not know.

7. And yet Paul says, Ἵ do not know” (2 Cor 12.2), on account of two things, as | think. One, as he said himself, lest because of the abundance of the revelations anyone should start thinking of him beyond what he saw (2 Cor 12.6-7). And regarding the day and the hour, he did not want to say divinely, “I know’, but he said according to the flesh, “I do not know’, for the sake of the flesh, which was ignorant, as | said, lest they should ask him further and then either he should have to frustrate the disciples by not speaking, or by speaking might do anything less than beneficial to them and us all. For if he does anything, he does that entirely for our sake, since for us “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14). And for us he said, “nor does the Son know”; and neither did he lie in saying this — for he said humanly, as man, “| do not know”, — nor did he give his disciples (the occasion) to force him to speak, for by saying “I do not know’, he stopped their inquiring.

4. For who, knowing the day of the end, would not do away with the rest of the time? But when one is ignorant, would he not be ready every day? For it was on this account that the Savior added by saying, “But be alert because you do not know in what day your Lord will come” (Mt 24.42), and “in such an hour as you do not think, the Son of man will come” (Lk 12.40). For the benefit that comes from ignorance did he say this, for in saying it, he wants that we should always be prepared. “For you”, he says, “do not know, but I, the Lord, know when | come, even though the Ario-maniacs do not expect me who am the Word of the Father’.

2. If then he is a mere man as other people, then let him, as a man, advance. But this is the idea of the (Paul) of Samosata, which essentially you presume as well, denying only the name because of men. But if he is God bearing flesh, since he truly is, and “the Word became flesh” (Jn 1.14), and being God descended on earth, what advance did he have (as) being equal to God? Or how did the Son increase, being always in the Father? For if he who was always in the Father, advanced, what then is there beyond the Father from which he should advance?

52. 1. For men, being creatures, are capable in a certain way of reaching forward and advancing in virtue. For Enoch was thus transposed (Gen 5.24; Heb 11.5), and Moses grew and was perfected (Ex 2.10-11), and Isaac, “by advancing became great” (Gen 26.13), and the apostle said that he “was reaching forward” day by day to what was before him (Phil 3.13). For each had where to advance, [+ looking gr.] to the scope before him. But the Son of God, who is one, where did he have to reach forward? For everything advances by looking at him, and he, being one in the only Father, from whom he does not reach forward either, [+ but gr.] in him abides all the time.

Ἀνθρώπων μὲν οὗν ἐστι τὸ προκόπτειν· ὁ δὲ τοῦ Θεοῦ Υἱὸς, ἐπεὶ προκόπτειν οὐκ εἶχε, τέλειος ὢν ἐν τῷ Πατρὶ, ἐταπείνωσεν ἑαυτὸν ὑπὲρ ἡμῶν, ἵνα ἐν τῷ ἐκεί¬ νου ταπεινῷ μᾶλλον ἡμεῖς αὐξῆσαι δυνηθῶμεν. Ἡ δὲ αὔξησις ἡμῶν οὐκ ἄλλη τίς ἐστιν ἢ τὸ ἀφίστασθαι μὲν τῶν αἰσθητῶν, εἰς αὐτὸν δὲ τὸν Λόγον γενέσθαι· ἐπεὶ καὶ τὸ ἐκείνου ταπεινὸν οὐδὲν ἕτερόν ἐστιν ἢ τὸ λαβεῖν τὴν ἡμετέραν σάρκα Οὐκ ἄρα ὁ Λόγος ἦν, ᾗ Λόγος ἐστὶν, ὁ προκόπτων, ὁ τέλειος ἐκ τελείου ὠ̔̀ν τοῦ Πατρὸς, ὁ μηδενὸς δεόμενος, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοὺς ἅλλους εἰς προκοπὴν ἀνάγων. Ἀλλὸ ἀνθρωπίνως εἵρηται καὶ ἐνταῦθα τὸ προκόπτειν· ἐπεὶ καὶ τῶν ἀνθρώπων ἐστὶ πάλιν ἡ προκοπή
2. To men then is proper to advance, but the Son of God, since he did not have anywhere to advance, being perfect in the Father, humbled himself for us (Phil 2.8), so that in his humbling, we rather might be able to increase. And our increase is no other than departing from things sensible in order to come to be in the Word himself, because his humbling is nothing else than taking our flesh. It was not then the Word, considered as the Word, [+ who gr.] advanced, (who is) perfect from the perfect Father, (who) requires nothing, but brings forward others to an advance. But it is humanly said here too that he advanced, because it is proper to man to advance.

3. For thus the evangelist too, speaking with cautious attention, included stature

тако. съ ѻблѭдаінѡ ѻпытаниемъ глаголѧ. къ спѣаниіѫ раштеlние причеталъ єсть. слово же и б҃ъ раштениемъ не мѣритьсѧ. нъ тѣлⷭ҇є́мь єсть раштєние. тѣлови же єсть спѣание. томѫ бо спѣѭштꙋ. спѣашесѧ въ немъ. и ꙗвление божьства его видѧштиимъ. Καὶ γὰρ καὶ ὁ εὐαγγελιστὴς οὕτω μετὰ ἀκριβοῦς τῆς παρατηρήσεως λέγων, τι προκοπῇ συνῆψε τὴν ἡλικίαν· Λόγος δὲ καὶ Θεὸς ἡλικίᾳ οὐ μετρεῖται, ἀλλὰ τῶν σωμάτων εἰσὶν αἱ ἡλικίαι. in the advance. But (as) Word and God, he is not measured by stature; (advancing in) stature is proper to bodies. Of the body then is the advance, for when it advanced, in it advanced also the manifestation of the Godhead to those who saw it.

3. But again, | would like you to give (me) an answer too, for all your statements need to be countered with the corresponding arguments. If the one who speaks is mere man, let him weep (and) let him fear death as man. (But) if he is the Word in flesh — for one must not be reluctant to say the same thing all the time, — who did he have to fear being God? Or why should he fear death, who was himself life and was saving others from death? Or how, while saying, “do not fear the one who kills the body” (Lk 12.4; Mt 10.28), should he himself fear? And how saying to Abraham, “Do not fear, for | am with you” (Gen 26.24) and urging Moses against Pharaoh (Ex 3.10), and saying to the son of Nun, “Be strong and courageous’ (Josh 1.6), did he himself fear Herod and Pontius (Pilate)? 4. It seems to me that these Christ-contenders have mastered a great deal of shamelessness and blasphemy. And when they hear, “I and the Father are one” (Jn 10.30), they force a distorted meaning and consider separating the unity of the Father and the Son. And when they hear that he wept, and sweated, and suffered, they do not look at the body, but because of this (they) rank in the creation him, through whom the creation was made. In what way then do they differ from the Jews? For just as those (Jews) blasphemed by ascribing God’s works to Beelzebub (Mt 12.24), so also will these, ranking with the creatures the Lord who made them, take the same judgment as theirs without mercy.

3. If then he wept and was troubled, it was not the Word, if considered as the Word, who wept and was troubled, but it belonged to the flesh. And if too he prayed that the cup might pass away (Mt 26.39), it was not the Godhead that feared, but this affection too belonged to the manhood. And as for the statement, “Why have you forsaken me”? (Mt 27.46), again, as it was said before, even though he did not suffer, for the Word was impassible, it is in his regard that this (statement) is spoken by the evangelists, since he became man, and these things are done and said as from a man, so that he might also lighten these things of the flesh and make it free from them.

57. 1. And concerning his statement, “If it is possible, let this cup pass” (Mt 26.39), let them understand that he said this rebuking Peter, [+ saying gr], “you are not thinking of what is God's” (Mt 16.23; Mk 8.33). For he desired what he denied, and for that very purpose he had come. But while it belonged to him to come [to will gr.] — for he came for it, — it was proper to the flesh to fear. Therefore, as man he utters such a statement, and yet again both were said by the same in order to indicate that he was God. He himself wanted (it), but when he became man, he (began) to have a flesh subject to fear. By way of it (i.e. flesh), he combined his own will with human weakness, so that destroying this (affection) too he might make man again bold in face of death.
6. For behold, when he said, “Why have you forsaken me”? (Mt 27.46), the Father showed that he was always with him, even then. For if the earth [For the earth gr.], knowing its Lord who spoke, immediately trembled, and the veil was torn apart, and the sun was hidden, and the rocks fell to pieces, and the graves, as | said, broke open, and the dead in them arose (Mt 27.51-54). And what is marvelous, those who stood there at that time and had before denied him, afterward, seeing these (signs), confessed that “truly he was the Son of God” (Mt 27.54).

3. How then is it not absurd indeed to marvel over the courage of the servants of the Word, yet to say that the Word himself feared, for whose sake they actually despised death? But through that enduring determination and courage of the holy martyrs is shown that the Godhead did not fear, but the Savior took (it) away by our fear [took away our fear gr.]. For as he abolished death by death, and by human means took away all things human, so also by the supposed fear did he take away our fear and made it such that men should no longer fear death. What he was saying, that same thing he was also doing.
Еже бо въꙁꙑмѧстисѧ свое єсть плъти. а єже власть имѣти положити и въꙁѧти. 6. Regarding this too speaks David in the Psalms, “You will not leave my soul in hades, nor will you give your holy one to see decay” (Ps 15.10). For it was fitting for the flesh, corruptible as it was, to no longer remain mortal after its own nature, but on account of the Word who had clothed himself with it, to remain incorruptible. For as he, having come in our body, conformed (himself) with our condition, so also, we, receiving him, partake of his immortality.

If the Christ-contenders were to think about this in such a way and understood the ecclesiastical sense as an anchor for the faith, they would not have fallen into trouble with the faith (1 Tim 1.19), nor been so shameless as to even resist those who want to restore them from their fall, nor been enemies with those who are admonishing them for piety. But, as it can be seen, a heretic is a deceiving thing in truth, and he has a heart that is totally depraved in evil faith.

Μετὰ γὰρ τοὺς τοσούτους κατʼ αὐτῶν ἐλέγχους, ἐφ᾽ οἷς καὶ ὁ διάβολος αὐτὸς ὁ τούτων πατὴρ ἐν τραπεὶς ἂν ἀπῆλθεν ὀπίσω, πάλιν ὡς ἀπὸ στρεβλῆς τῆς καρδίας αὐτῶν ἐπινοοῦντες γογγύζουσι, καὶ τοῖς μὲν ψιθυρίζουσι, τοῖς δὲ, ὡς κώνωπες, περιβομβοῦσι λέγοντες· Ἑστω, ταῦτα οὕτως ἕρμηνεύετε, καὶ νικᾶτε τοῖς λογισμοῖς καὶ ταῖς ἀποδείξεσιν ἀλλὰ δεῖ λέγειν βουλήσει καὶ θελήσει γεγενῆσθαι τὸ γϊὸν ὑπὸ τοῦ Πατρός. 59. 1. After so many refutations against them, at which even the devil (as) their father (Jn 8.44), has been abashed and gone back, again, as from their perverse heart, they mutter forth other ideas; to some they whisper and to some they buzz like mosquitoes, saying, “Be it so as you interpret these places, and let us have you win in (your) reasoning and points, (but) still you must say that the Son was begotten from [the Son received being from gr.] the Father by will and pleasure”. For in such a way they deceive many, putting forward the will and the pleasure of God.


[p. 201] {#po.58.p201}
и сеже, аште кто отъ вѣрѫѭжштий правь, просто би глаголалъ не би ничєсоⷨ въꙁымнѣти о глаголємѣемь. прѣдєлѣѭштꙋ праівовѣрнꙋоумѫ оумоу. иꙁношениоу простоуlоумꙋ, глаголъ. нъ єльмажє отъ ерєтикъ єсты гласъ. ꙁазираема же сѫть словєса ерєтикъ. ꙗкоже пишєть · Окрьмлѧѭть же нечыстивии льсти. и словєса нечьстивъихъ сѫты льстива. аште и тъчиѭ помаваѭть. истлѣно бо имѫть срѣⷣцє · Воле же да и се глаголемое опытаимъ. єда како въсѣхъ Обличєни бывꙑше. оуже ꙗкоже у́дры. новѣе словєсьце ꙋмыслишѧ. да тѣмъ новословєсиємь, и личнаа҇ написаниѧ свое нечьстие пакы инако въсѣѭть. тожде бо наꙁнаменѫєть глаголѧи. воілє рождьнъ єсть сынъ. и глаголѧи бѣ нѣкъгда єгда не бѣ. и отъ не сѫштий бысть г҃ь, и ꙁданїе єⷭ҇. Ταὐτὸν γὰρ σημαίνει ὁ λέγων, Βουλήσει γέγονεν ὁ Yἱὸς, καὶ ὁ λέγων Ἡν ποτε ὅτε οὐκ ῆν, καὶ, Ἐξ οὐκ ὅντων γέγονεν ὁ Yιὸς, καὶ κτίσμα ἐστίν
2. And if anyone who keeps the right faith were to say this in simplicity, there would be no reason to be suspicious of the statement, (for) the orthodox sense would prevail over the simple pronouncement of the expression. But since the expression is from the heretics and the words of heretics are suspicious, [+ and gr.] as it is written, “impious men administer deceits” (Prov 12.5), and “the words of the impious are deceitful” (Prov 12.6), even though they only make signs, for they have a depraved heart, behold, let us examine this expression too, lest, though convicted on all sides, as hydras, they come up with a new wording and by that new wording and masked writing, they sow again impiety in another way. For he who says, “The Son was begotten at the will (of God)”, signifies the same thing as the one who says, “there was once when he was not”, and “the Lord came to be out of nothing”, and “he is a creature”.

to convey their meaning in another way, putting forth the term “will”, as [+ cuttlefish their gr.] blackness, in order to confuse the simple that way, and not to forget their own heresy. For where does (the phrase) “by will and pleasure” come from? Or from what kind of Scripture do they bring them up? Let them say, those who are (so) suspicious in what they say and who invent ill piety!

60. 1. (Everyone and) everywhere is telling us of the being of the Word, but nowhere of his being “from the will”, nor at all does anyone say that he was made. And Asterius, who speaks the heresy with them, joins, when he writes as follows, “For if it is unworthy of the Framer of everything to make at pleasure, let his being pleased be taken away equally if [equally in gr.] the case of all, so that his position be preserved intact. If it fits for God to will, then let this better way belong to him in the case of the first offspring. For it is not possible that it should fit one and the same God to make things at his pleasure, and not at his will also”.

4. And this again the apostle signifies when he writes to Thessalonica, “for this is God's will regarding you in Christ Jesus” (1 Thess 5.18). If the one in whom he makes, in him also is the will, and in Christ is the pleasure of the Father, how can he himself come into being by pleasure and will? For if he himself came to be, according to you, it is necessary that the will concerning him would consist in some other Word, through whom he then comes to be. For it has been shown that God's will is not in the things that come into being, but in him through whom and in whom all things made are brought to be (Col 1.16).

5. Since it is the same to say, “by will” and “there was when he was not”, let them be satisfied with saying, “there was when he was not’, so that feeling shame in that through this they signify time, they may understand that by saying “by will’, they imply years preceding the Son. For will precedes things that never existed before, as in the case of all creatures. But if the Word is the Framer of creation, and is [+ with gr.] the Father, how can the will precede the one who is eternal as if he were not? For if will precedes, how is it that through him is everything? (Jn 1.3).

62. 1. If then there is another Word of God, then the Son should have come to be by a word. But if there is not, for that is how it is, but everything by him has come to be, which the Father has willed, how does this not show the many-headed deception of these men? As being ashamed at saying, “work”, and “creature”, and “God’s Word did not exist before it was begotten”, they say in another way again that he is a creature, putting forward “will”, and saying, “If he did not come to be by will, then God had a Son by necessity and without willing”. And who is it (then) who imposes necessity on him, you deceiving men, who draw everything (for the benefit of) your heresy?

2. For what is contrary to will, that they see, but what is greater and transcendent, that they do not see. For just as that which falls outside the will is contrary to will, so also that which is according to nature transcends and precedes the will. Based on desire, one makes a house, but by nature he begets a son; and the thing made began to come into being at will and is outside of the maker, but the son is proper offspring of the Father’s essence, and is not outside of him.

3. Therefore, neither does he counsel about him, lest he be perceived as counseling about himself. For as far as the Son is above the creature, by so much does what is by nature is above the will. Let them also say this again — (here) against their shamelessness we would like to make one (further) inquiry to them, bold as it is, but with a view to good faith Have mercy (on us), oh Lord! — the Father himself, does he exist, first having counseled, then being pleased, or before counseling? 3. For it is also sufficient even just on hearing of the Word to know and understand that he who is God not by will, has his own Word not by will but by nature. For he seems to be considering himself, who counsels about what is proper to his essence.

ὅτι ὁ μὴ ἐκ βουλήσεως ὑπάρχων Θεὸς οὐ βουλήσει, ἀλλὰ φύσει τὸν ἵδιον ἔχε Λόγον. 4. There being then much blasphemy in such a thought, it will be in accord with good faith then to point out that things originate have come to be “by favor and will”, but the Son is not a production of will, nor has he come after, as the creation, but is by nature the proper offspring of God’s essence. For being the proper Word of the Father, he does not allow to suggest any will (as existing) before himself, since he is himself the Father's living counsel, and framing power of everything that is the Father's.
Мнѡгꙋ оубо хоулєниѭ сѫштꙋ въ таковѣемь съмышлєнии. блаіговѣрно оубо ꙋкаꙁано бѫдєть. ꙗко бышьнаѧ блаіговоленїемъ и волеѭ бышѧ. сынъ же нѣсть волѧ съдѣтєлие. прибывъ ꙗкоⷤ тварь. нъ сѫтьствоⷩ єстьства свои поро҇. небонъ свое сѫштє слово отцѫ. не даєтъ прьвѣе сєбѣ никакꙋ же волѭ съвѣштавати. самъ сы съвѣтъ живъ отьчь. и сила съдѣтельна отчааго всего. Πολλῆς τοίνυν δυσφημίας οὕσης ἐν τῇ τοιαύτῃ φρονήσει, εὐσεβῶς ἂν λεχθείη ὅτι τὸ μὲν γενητὰ εὐδοκίᾳ καὶ βουλήσει γέγονεν, ὁ δὲ Yἱὸς οὐ θελήματός ἐστι δημιούργημα ἐπιγεγονὼς, καθάπερ ἡ κτίσις, ἀλλὰ φύσει τῆς οὐσίας ἵδιον γέν- νημα. Καὶ γὰρ ἵδιος ὣν Λόγος τοῦ Πατρὸς, οὐκ ἐᾷ πρὸ ἑαυτοῦ λογίσασθαί τινο βούλησιν, αὐτὸς ὣν βουλὴ ζῶσα τοῦ Πατρὸς, καὶ δύναμις, καὶ δημιουργὸς τῶν δοκούντων τῷ Πατρί.
5. And this is what he says of himself in the Proverbs, “Counsel and security are mine, mine is wisdom, mine is strength” (Prov 8.14). For as, although he himself was the “Wisdom” through which he made the heavens, and he himself “strength and power” — for Christ is “God’s power and God's wisdom” (1 Cor 1.24), — he has now made an adjustment, saying, “mine is wisdom”, and “strength is mine”. Thus, while he says, “mine is counsel’, he is himself indeed the living counsel of the Father, as we have learned from the prophet also, that he becomes “the angel of great counsel” (Isa 9.5-6), and was called the will of the Father (Isa 62.4). This is the way how we must rebuke them, considering human (illustrations) with regard to God.

65. 1. Since they say that the Son is by will, it should fit them to say also that he came to be by wisdom. For | consider wisdom and will to be the same. For what one counsels, about that also he thinks; and what he thinks about, that also he counsels. The Savior himself has joined it all as if in brotherhood, saying, “Counsel and security are mine, mine is strength” (Prov 8.14). For as strength and security are the same — for they are identical in power, — so we may say that wisdom and will are the same, which is the Lord.

3. Let them hear what Simon the Sorcerer heard, “(May) the impiety of Valentinus perish with you” (Acts 8.20). And rather let everyone listen to Solomon who says that the Word is Wisdom and reason. For he says, “The Lord by Wisdom founded the earth, by reason he prepared the heavens” (Prov 3.19). So, in the Psalms, “by the Word of the Lord were the heavens established” (Ps 32.6). And as by the Word the heavens were established, so “he has done whatever pleased him” (Ps 134.6). And as the apostle writes to Thessalonians, “the will of God is in Christ Jesus” (1 Thess 5.18). The Son is of the invisible God, he is the Word and the Wisdom, he is the reason and counsel, and in him is the pleasure of the Father. He is truth and light and power of the Father.

4. But if the will of God is wisdom and reason, and the Son is Wisdom, then we should be careful not to say that the Son is “by will’, (for it) is the same as to say that Wisdom has come into being by wisdom, and the Son is made in a son, and the Word is created through the word. This is slavish with regard to God [This opposes God gr.] and contradicts the Scriptures that are from him.

2. For just as he did not begin to be good “from will”, nor yet is good without will and without pleasure — for what he is, that also he wants, — so also (it is with the fact) that the Son should be, even though it did not happen “from will”, yet it was not without his pleasure nor outside of his will. For just as (God) desires his own hypostasis, so also the Son, being proper to his essence, is not without his pleasure.

But to say next to the Son [of the Son gr.], “he might not have been’ is evil faith, an audacity that reaches even to the essence of the Father, as if what is said to be his own might not have been. For it is equal to saying, “The Father might not have been good”. But as the Father is always good by nature, so he is always generative by nature, 6. and to say, “The Father desires the Son”, and “the Son desires the Father” does not show the preceding will, but speaks of genuineness of nature, and propriety and likeness of essence.

67. 1. Therefore, do not say that the Son is a production of pleasure, nor introduce the teaching of Valentinus into the Church. But let him be the living counsel, and offspring in truth and nature, as the radiance from the light.
For as in the case of the radiance [+ and gr_] light, one might say that there is no will preceding radiance in the light, but it is its natural offspring, at the pleasure of the light which begot it, not by counsel of the will, but in nature and truth, so also with regard to the Father and with regard to the Son, everyone might rightly say that the Father loves and desires the Son, and the Son loves and desires the Father.

2. For thus did the Father say, “My heart uttered a good Word” (Ps 44.2), and the Son accordingly, “I am in the Father and the Father in me” (Jn 14.10). But if the Word is in the heart, where is will? And if the Son is in the Father, where is pleasure? And he is will himself, how is will in will? For this is absurd, lest the Word come into being in a word, and the Son in a son, and Wisdom in a wisdom, as has been many times said. For the Son is the Father's all, and nothing was in the Father before the Word, but in the Word is will also, and through him things of will come into completion, as holy Scriptures teach.


[p. 227] {#po.58.p227}
Хотѣахъ же нєчьстивыа. въ такое скотьство въпадъшѧ. и о воли съвѣштаѫшта нынє въпраішати. оуже не жены ихъ раждаѭштѧѧ. ꙗжє ти прьвѣє въпрашаахѫ глаголѭштє. аште имѣла єси сынъ прьвѣе даже не родиши. нъ отца и рєшти имъ. прьвѣє ли волѧ аштє бываєтє отци. или по сѫтьствѫ вашеѧ волѧ. или вашего сѫтьства и єстьства сѫть подобьна чѧда. да понѣ отꙑ родитєль посрамѧтьсѧ. отъ нихъжє въꙁѧтиє бытиа приѧшѧ. и ѻтъ нихꙿжє надѣашⷭ҇ѧ имѣти раꙁоумъ. Ἐβουλόμην δὲ τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς οὕτως εἰς ἀλογίαν πεπτωκότας, καὶ περὶ βουλή σεως σκεπτομένους, νῦν ἐρωτῆσαι μηκέτι τὰς τικτούσας αὐτῶν γυναῖκας, ἃς οὗτο σεως σκεπτομένους, νῦν ἐρωτῆσαι μηκέτι τὰς τικτούσας αὐτῶν γυναῖκας, ἃς οῦτοι πρότερον ἠρώτων λέγοντες, Εὶ εἶχες υἱὸν, πρὶν γεννήσης; ἀλλὰ τοὺς πατέρας, καὶ πρότερον ἠρώτων λέγοντες, Εὶ εῖχες υἱὸν, πρὶν γεννήσης; ἀλλὰ τοὺς πατέρας, κα εἰπεῖν αὐτοῖς· Πότερον βουλευόμενοι γίνεσθε πατέρες, ἡ κατὰ φύσιν καὶ τῆς ὑμῶν βουλήσεως; Ἡ̓ τῆς φύσεως καὶ οὐσίας ὑμῶν ἐστιν ὅμοια τὰ τέκνα; ἵνα κὰν παρὰ τῶν γονέων ἐντραπῶσι, παρ᾽ ὧν τὸ λῆμμα τῆς γενέσεως ἀπήτησαν, καὶ παρ ὦν ἥλπι- σαν ἔχειν τὴν γνῶσιν.

And | would want that the impious men, having now fallen into such irrationality as to be considering will, now no longer ask their childbearing women — whom they used to ask previously, “Did you have a son before begetting him”? — but that they would (ask) the father [fathers gr.] and say to them, “Do you become fathers prior to (your) will, or by nature of your will’? Or “Are your children like your nature and essence”? (They should ask this), so that even from fathers they may feel ashamed, from whom they took the idea of origin, and from whom they hoped to have knowledge (on this issue).
отъвѣштаѭть бо имъ, ихже раждаємы, не волѧ. нъ наши сѫть подоібни. ни прѣждє съвѣштавъшє бываємъ родителе. нъ сѫтьства своє єсть єже раждати, ꙁаінежє и мы, родивъшиихъ єсмь ѻбраꙁи. или оубо да ꙁаꙁираѭть сєбѣ сами. и да прѣстанѫть жены въꙑпрашаѭжште о сынѣ божии. или и да оучѧтьсѧ отъ нихъ, ꙗко не раждаетьсѧ сынъ волєѭ, нꙑ сѫтьствомь и сⷦтиноѭ. 4. For they will reply to them, “What we beget is like not our will, but like ourselves, nor did we become parents by previous counsel, but to beget is proper to our nature, since we too are images of those who begot us”. Either then let them rebuke themselves, and stop asking women about the Son of God, or let them learn from them that the Son is begotten not by will, but in nature and truth.

6. For truth is loving toward men and cries out all the time, “If you do not believe me because | was clothed in the body, then believe my works, so that you may understand that ‘I am in the Father and the Father in me’ (Jn 19.38), and ‘I and the Father are one’ (Jn 10.30), and ‘he who has seen me has seen the Father” (Jn 14.10). But the Lord according to his custom is loving toward men, and wants that all who have fallen would be restored (Ps 145.8), as the singing of David says.

7. But the impious men do not want to hear the Lord's voice, nor do they bear to see Christ to be acknowledged by all as God and God's Son. They, pitiful as they are, go about as beetles, seeking with their father the devil pretexts for impiety.
8. What pretext then, and from where would they make it up again, if not by simply borrowing the blasphemies of Jews and Caiaphas, and taking the godlessness from Greeks? For the divine Scriptures are closed to them, and in every way, they are rebuked as mindless and contenders against Christ.
